<!-- conjecture-1: body_md -->
\label{Conj:MNW-examples}
    The solutions $u_{\varepsilon_i}$ constructed by Malchiodi, Ni and Wei in \cite{Malchiodi2007} and \cite{Malchiodi2007a} have unbounded Morse index as $\varepsilon_i \to 0$.
<!-- end conjecture-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{T:main-theorem}
    Let $\varepsilon_i \to 0$  and suppose $\{u_{\varepsilon_i}\}$ is a sequence of stable solutions to the Allen-Cahn equation \eqref{E:Allen-Cahn-intro} with $E_{\varepsilon_i}(u_{\varepsilon_i})\leq E_0$ and $\|u_{\varepsilon_i}\|_{L^\infty(M)} \leq C_0$ for some constants $E_0,C_0>0$. Then, for any limit-interface $V$ obtained from a subsequential limit of $V^{\varepsilon_i}$ as above, the following hold:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item[(1)] $V$ is a curvature varifold with boundary in the sense of Mantegazza (c.f. Definition \ref{D:curvature-varifolds})
        \item[(2)] $V$ satisfies a stability-type inequality: for any $\psi \in C^1_c(M)$, we have
\[
    \int |\mathbf{B}_V|^2\psi^2 \, d\|V\| \leq \int |\nabla\psi|^2 \, d\|V\| + C_M \int \psi^2 \, d\|V\| + C_M \int \psi^2 \, d \|\delta V\|_{sing},
\]
where $\mathbf{B}_V$ is the generalized second fundamental form of $V$ (c.f. Definition \ref{D:curvature-varifolds})  and $C_M \geq 0$ is a geometric constant depending only on $M$.
\item[(3)] $V$ is stationary across the ``touching set'', i.e. the first variation of $V$ is given by
\[
    \delta V(X)= \int X \cdot \nu \; d \|\delta V\|_{sing} 
\]
where $X$ is any $C^1$ vector field on $M$ which is not necessarily tangential to $\partial M$.
\item[(4)] $V$ admits no classical tangent cones except possibly on an $\mathcal{H}^{n-2}$-null set of $\operatorname{spt} \|V\|$.
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{R:minimal-across-touching-set}
    To understand (3), note that if $V$ is the unit-density varifold corresponding to the domain boundary $\partial M$, then it is automatically a free boundary stationary varifold with free boundary in $\partial M$ (c.f. Definition \ref{D:free-boundary-varifold}). However, it will satisfy (3) only when $\partial M$  is itself a minimal hypersurface (since in this case $\|\delta V\|_{sing}=0$ as $\partial M$ itself has no boundary). Therefore, (3) is saying in a weak sense (which can be made precise by \eqref{E:1st-variation-fbv-stationary}) the limit-interface $V$ must be ``minimal'' on the part where it leans onto $\partial M$ (c.f. Remark \ref{R:stationarity-across-touching}).
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{C:main-corollary}
    Conjecture \ref{Conj:MNW-examples} is true.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
(4) is the free boundary analogue of a fundamental result of Tonegawa-Wickramasekera \cite[Proposition 3.2]{Tonegawa2012} for the interior case in the Euclidean setting. Here, we extend their result up to the boundary and in the Riemannian setting. In another paper \cite{Bellettini-Li-Parise-Sarnataro} of the same authors with Bellettini, we established the compactness and regularity theory for stable free boundary minimal hypersurfaces under an a-priori smallness assumption on the singular set in the spirit of Schoen-Simon (c.f. \cite{SchoenSimon81} and \cite{Bellettini2025}). In our forthcoming work, where we develop the full free boundary optimal regularity of Wickramasekera's far-reaching interior compactness and regularity theory \cite{Wick14}, (3) is the key variational hypothesis and (4) will be used to verify the main structural hypothesis on the absence of classical singularities.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{R:rectifiability}
Note that the definition of a varifold does not really require the Riemannian metric $g$. In fact, the notion of rectifiability and integer-rectifiability is independent of the choice of $g$. The reason is that (integer) rectifiability is bi-Lipschitz invariant and any Riemannian metric is infinitesimally isometric to the flat Euclidean space. In particular, if $F:U \to \widetilde{U}$ is a $C^1$ proper map between two open sets of Riemannian manifolds, then the pushforward map $F_\sharp:\mathbf{V}_{n-1}(U) \to \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(\widetilde{U})$, as defined in \cite[Section 39]{Simon1983}, satisfies $F_\sharp(\mathbf{RV}_{n-1}(U)) \subset \mathbf{RV}_{n-1}(U)$ and $F_\sharp(\mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(U)) \subset \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(U)$ (c.f. \cite[3.5(3)]{Allard1972} in the context of isometric embedding or \cite[I.1 (13)]{Almgren1976}).
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
In contrast to Remark \ref{R:rectifiability}, the notion of first variation $\delta V$ is a geometric quantity that depends on the Riemannian metric $g$. This is intuitively clear as the first variation represents in a generalized sense the mean curvature vector and the boundary with its outward unit normal which are both geometric quantities. In particular, the notion of locally bounded first variation is not a diffeomorphism invariant. This is more evident when there is a singular part of the first variation. For example, consider the one-dimensional rectifiable varifold in $\mathbb{R}^2$ in \cite[Example 2, p.256]{Allard-Almgren1973} which is stationary in a ball centred at the origin, if we apply a diffeomorphism to this ball which preserves the origin but distorts the angles at all the triple junctions, we will end up with a rectifiable varifold with unbounded first variation near the origin.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
Note that one can also define the set of tangent varifolds via isometric embedding as in \cite{Allard1972}. If the metric $g$ is regular enough (say $C^{2,\alpha}$ regularity), the two notions of tangent varifolds are equivalent. 
                                                                                                       
    Since we chose to avoid using Nash's isometric embeddeding in this paper, we adopt the above intrinsic definition instead. Another formulation is to consider the rescaling of the metric $r_i^{-2}g$ as done in \cite{LiPariseSarnataro2023} which is again equivalent as $d\exp_x|_0$ is the identity map of $T_x\widetilde{M}$.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{D:free-boundary-varifold}
Given a smoothly\footnote{It is sufficient to assume $C^2$ regularity of $S$.} embedded hypersurface $S \subset U$ oriented by the (outward) unit normal $\nu_S$, a varifold $V \in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(U)$ is said to have free boundary in $S$ \footnote{As in \cite{Edelen2020,Masi2020}, this definition implicitly assumes that $\delta V$ has locally bounded first variation with $\|\delta V\|_{sing}=0$ away from the support hypersurface $S$.} if there exists a $\|V\|$-measurable vector field $H^{tan}_V \in L^1_{loc}(U;\|V\|)$ and $H^{tan}_V(x) \cdot \nu_S (x) =0$ for $\|V\|$-a.e. $x \in S$, satisfying
\begin{equation}
\label{E:free-boundary-varifold}
\delta V(X)= - \int_U X(x)\cdot H^{tan}_V(x) \; d\|V\|(x) \qquad \text{$\forall$ $X \in \mathfrak{X}_c(U)$ such that $X \cdot \nu_S=0$ on $S$.}
\end{equation}
If $\delta V(X)=0$ for all $X \in \mathfrak{X}_c(U)$ such that $X \cdot \nu_S=0$ on $S$ (in other words, $H^{tan}_V=0$ $\|V\|$-a.e.), we say that $V$ is free boundary stationary with free boundary in $S$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
Unlike in \cite{Masi2020} (and implicitly in \cite{Edelen2020}), according to our Definition \ref{D:free-boundary-varifold}, the varifold $V$ is not required to lie on one side (determined by the unit normal $\nu_S$) of the support hypersurface $S$. Although most of the time we will only consider $S=\partial \Omega \cap U$ and $V \in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(U)$ with $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \subset \overline{\Omega} \cap U$, having this extra flexibility is convenient for us in certain rescaling arguments in the Riemannian setting as will be evident in the next subsection.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{P:free-boundary-characterization}
Let $V \in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(U)$. Then $V$ has free boundary in $S$ if and only if $V$ has locally bounded first variation in $U$ with $\operatorname{spt} \|\delta V\|_{sing} \subset S$ and $\eta_V(x)=\pm \nu_S(x)$ for $\|\delta V\|_{sing}$-a.e. $x \in S$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
First, note that the same constancy lemma \cite[Lemma 3.1]{Masi2020} and the main theorem \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Masi2020} hold in the Riemannian setting by \eqref{E:dist-estimates}. Therefore, the proposition follows immediately as in \cite[Corollary 4.7]{Masi2020} from the following formula:
\begin{equation} 
\label{E:1st-variation-fbv-refined}
\delta V(X) = - \int_{U} X(x)\cdot (H^{tan}_V(x) + H_S (x)) \; d \|V\|(x) + \int_{S} X(x)\cdot \eta_V(x) \; d\|\delta V\|_{sing}(x), 
\end{equation} 
for all $X \in \mathfrak{X}_c(U)$, where $H_S$ is the mean curvature vector of $S$ (which by convention vanishes outside $S$) and $\eta_V(x)=\pm \nu_S(x)$ for $\|\delta V\|_{sing}$-a.e. $x$. Note that $H^{tan}_V$ and $H_S$ are mutually orthogonal for $\|V\|$-a.e. $x$. We want to remark that we have $\eta_V=\nu_S$ only when $V$ is assumed to be supported on one side of $S$ (c.f. the last paragraph in Step 4 of the proof of Theorem 1.1 in \cite{Masi2020}). In general, we only have $\eta_V=\pm \nu_S$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
Given the main results in \cite{LiPariseSarnataro2023} (see Theorem \ref{T:old-main}), we shall only be concerned with free boundary varifolds that are also integral, we shall from now on implicitly make that assumption, even though it is not strictly necessary for some of the general results in this section. We shall refer to \cite{Masi2020} for a more general treatment for free boundary varifolds which are not necessarily integral (or even rectifiable).
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{L:trace-inequality}
    Let $V \in \mathbf{V}_{n - 1}(U)$ be a varifold with free boundary in $\partial \Omega \cap U$ and $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \subset \overline{\Omega} \cap U$. For any nonnegative $f \in C_{c}^{1}(U)$, we have 
    \begin{equation} 
    \label{equation: refined isop general I}
        \int f \; d\|\delta V\|_{sing} \leq C_3 \int \left( ( 1 + | H^{tan}_V| ) f 
        + |\nabla f| \right) \; d\|V \|
    \end{equation}
    where $C_3=C_3(n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0)>0$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
We will basically follow the arguments in \cite[Subsection 3.2]{Masi2020} adapted to the local Riemannian setting. Fix $f \in C^1_c(U)$. Since $f$ has compact support in $U$, a straightforward covering argument shows that it suffices to establish \eqref{equation: refined isop general I} locally around any $x_0 \in \partial \Omega \cap U$. Let $r=\operatorname{dist}_g(\cdot,x_0)$ and $d=\operatorname{dist}_g(\cdot,\partial \Omega)$. We test \eqref{E:1st-variation-fbv-refined} with the compactly-supported vector field 
\[ X(x) = - f(x) \chi\left(\frac{r(x)}{R} \right) \nabla d(x)\]
where $\chi$ is the cut-off function defined in Section \ref{SS:cutoff}, and $R = \frac{1}{2} \min\{ i_0,\frac{1}{2\kappa_0}\}$. Thus, we have the following
    \begin{align*}
        \begin{split}
            \int_{B_{R/2}(x_0)} f(x) \; & d\|\delta V\|_{sing}(x)  \leq \int f(x) \chi\left(\frac{r(x)}{R} \right)  \; d\|\delta V\|_{sing}(x) = \int X(x) \cdot \nu(x)  \; d\|\delta V\|_{sing}(x) \\
            & = \int \mathrm{div}_S X(x) \; dV(x, S) + \int X(x)\cdot \left(H^{tan}_V(x) + H_{\partial \Omega} (x) \right) \; d \|V\| (x).
        \end{split}
    \end{align*}
On the other hand, a direct computation shows $\mathrm{div}_S X(x)$ is equal to
    \begin{equation*}
        - f(x) \chi^\prime\left(\frac{r(x)}{R} \right) \frac{1}{R} \left( S(\nabla r) \cdot \nabla d\right)(x) - f(x) \chi\left( \frac{r(x)}{R} \right) \mathrm{div}_S \nabla d(x) - \chi\left( \frac{r(x)}{R} \right) \left( \nabla d \cdot S(\nabla f) \right) (x). 
    \end{equation*}
Note that $| \mathrm{div}_S \nabla d | \leq (n-1)C_1(n,k_0,\kappa_0)$ in $B_R(x_0)$ by \eqref{E:dist-estimates}. Furthermore, $| H_{\partial \Omega} | \leq (n-1)\kappa_0$ by \eqref{E:ball-curvatures}. Therefore, we can bound
    \begin{align*}
        - \int f(x) \chi\left( \frac{r(x)}{R} \right) & \mathrm{div}_S \nabla d(x) \; dV(x, S)  - \int f(x) \chi\left(\frac{r(x)}{R} \right) \nabla d(x) \cdot \left( H^{tan}_V(x) + H_{\partial \Omega}(x) \right)   d \| V \|(x) \\
        & \leq C(n,k_0,\kappa_0) \int_{B_R(x_0)} \left(1 +  |H^{tan}_V(x)| \right)f(x) \; d\| V \|(x). 
    \end{align*}
The other terms appearing in $\mathrm{div}_S X(x)$ can be controlled by fixing a cutoff function such that $\| \chi^\prime \|_{L^\infty} \leq 3$ and the observation that $|\nabla r|=|\nabla d|=1$. This concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{C:free-bdy-trace-ineq}
For every $\eta >0$, there exists $C_4(\eta,n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0)>0$ such that the following holds: let $V \in \mathbf{V}_{n - 1}(U)$ be a varifold with free boundary in $\partial \Omega \cap U$ and $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \subset \overline{\Omega} \cap U$, for any $\psi \in C^1_c(U)$, we have
  \begin{equation}
        \int \psi^2\, d\|\delta V\|_{sing} \leq (1+\eta) \int |\nabla\psi|^2\,d\|V\| + C_4 \int \left( 1+ |H^{tan}_V| \right) \psi^2 \, d\|V\|. 
    \end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{L:reflection stationary}
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          
    For any $V \in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $\operatorname{spt} V \subset \mathbb{R}^n_+$, define the doubled-varifold 
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{V} := V + (\theta_v)_\sharp V \in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(\mathbb{R}^n). 
    \end{equation*}
    If $V$ is free boundary stationary with free boundary in $\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$, then $\overline{V}$ is stationary in $\mathbb{R}^n$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{L:constancy lemma De Masi}
 Let $V \in \mathbf{V}_{n - 1}(U)$ be a varifold with free boundary in $\partial \Omega \cap U$. Then, for any $\phi \in C_c(G(U))$, we have
 \[ \int_{G(U)} \phi(x,S) \; dV(x,S) = \int_{\partial \Omega \cap U} \phi(x,T_x\partial \Omega) \Theta^{n-1}(\|V\|,x) \; d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(x) + \int_{G(U \setminus \partial \Omega)} \phi(x,S) \; dV(x,S).\]
 In particular, $\|V\| \, \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}_{\partial \Omega}= \Theta^{n-1}(\|V\|,\cdot) \; d \mathcal{H}^{n-1} \, \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}_{\partial \Omega}$ and $V$ is rectifiable along $\partial \Omega \cap U$ with 
    \begin{equation}
    \label{E:constancy-lemma}
        V \left( \{(x,S)\in G(U) \, | \, x \in \partial \Omega \cap U, S \neq T_x \partial \Omega \} \right)=0.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{L:stationarity planar tangents}
    Using the same notation as in Lemma \ref{L:reflection stationary}, and suppose $V\in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ is free boundary stationary with free boundary in $\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$.
    Assume, in addition, that $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \subset \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$. Then $V$ is stationary in $\mathbb{R}^n$ and thus $V=m |\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+|$ for some real constant $m >0$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
By the classical constancy theorem \cite[41.1]{Simon1983}, it suffices to show that $V$ is stationary in $\mathbb{R}^n$. We claim that $(\theta_v)_\sharp V=V$. Once the claim is established, we see from Lemma \ref{L:reflection stationary} that the doubled varifold $\overline{V}=V+(\theta_v)_\sharp V=2V$ is stationary in $\mathbb{R}^n$. This of course implies stationarity of $V=\frac{1}{2} \overline{V}$ in $\mathbb{R}^n$.

It remains to establish the claim that $(\theta_v)_\sharp V=V$. Since $v \in \mathbb{R}^n$ and $\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+=\{u\in \mathbb{R}^n \, | \, u \cdot v=0\}$, for all $x\in \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$ we have $\theta_v(x)=x$ and $D_x\theta_v$ acts as the identity map on $\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$. Moreover, notice that for any $(y,S) \in G(\mathbb{R}^n)$, the Jacobian (see \cite[\S 39]{Simon1983}) is given by $J_S \theta_v (y)=1$. Therefore, by the definition of pushforward of a varifold (see again \cite[\S 39]{Simon1983}), we have for all $f \in C^0_c (G(\mathbb{R}^n))$
    \begin{equation}
    \begin{split}
        \int_{G(\mathbb{R}^n)}f(z, T) \; d((\theta_v)_\sharp V)(z,T) &= \int_{G(\mathbb{R}^n)} f(\theta_v(y), D_y\theta_v(S))J_S\theta_v(S) \; dV(y, S)\\
        &=\int_{\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+ \times \mathbf{G}(n,n-1)}f(\theta_v(y), D_y\theta_v(S)) \; dV(y,S)\\
        &\qquad + \int_{G(\mathbb{R}^n \setminus \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+)} f(\theta_v(y), D_y\theta_v(S)) \; dV(y,S).
    \end{split}
    \end{equation}
    Using \eqref{E:constancy-lemma}, $\theta_v(y)=y$ for $y \in \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$ and the fact that $D_y\theta_v(S) \neq \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$ for all $y \in \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$, $S \neq \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$, we see that 
    \begin{equation}
        \int_{\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+ \times \mathbf{G}(n,n-1)}f(\theta_v(y), D_y\theta_v(S)) \; dV(y,S) =\int_{\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+ \times \{\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+\}} f(y, \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+) \; dV(y,S).
    \end{equation}
On the other hand, since $\operatorname{spt} \| V \| \subset \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$ by assumption, we have 
    \begin{equation}
        \int_{G(\mathbb{R}^n \setminus \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+)} f(\theta_v(y), D_y\theta_v(S)) \; dV(y,S) =0.
    \end{equation}
Combining all these and Lemma \ref{L:constancy lemma De Masi}, we have established the claim that $(\theta_v)_\sharp V=V$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{R:stationarity planar tangents}
In fact, the above result is local and the same statement holds when $\mathbb{R}^n$ (and respectively $\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$) is replaced by any open set $U \subset \mathbb{R}^n$ (and respectively $U \cap \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$) which is invariant under the reflection $\theta_v$.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{L:monotonicity limit varifold}
    For every $\Lambda \geq 0$, there exists $C_5(n, k_0, \kappa_0, \Lambda)>0$ such that for any varifold $V \in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(U)$ with free boundary in $\partial \Omega \cap U$, $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \subset \overline{\Omega} \cap U$ and $\|H^{tan}_V\|_{L^\infty(U)} \leq \Lambda$, the function
    \begin{equation}
    \label{E:monotonicity limit varifold}
        \rho \mapsto e^{C_5 \rho}\frac{\|V\|(B_\rho(x))}{\rho^{n-1}}
    \end{equation}
    is monotone increasing for $0<\rho< \frac{1}{2} \min \{ i_0, \frac{1}{\kappa_0}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{k_0}},2\operatorname{dist}_g(x,\partial U) \}$ when $x \in \partial \Omega \cap U$; and for $0< \rho <\frac{1}{2} \min \{ i_0, \frac{1}{\sqrt{k_0}}, 2\operatorname{dist}_g(x,\partial \Omega \cup \partial U) \} $ when $x \in U \setminus \partial \Omega$. In particular, the $(n-1)$-dimensional density
    \begin{equation}
        \Theta_V(x):=\lim_{\rho\to 0}\frac{\|V\|(B_\rho(x))}{\omega_{n-1}\rho^{n-1}}
    \end{equation}
    exists for all $x\in U$ (and vanishes outside $\operatorname{spt} \|V\|$).
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
The proof (in the Euclidean setting) is essentially contained in \cite[Section 4.5]{Masi2020}. We include the main steps here for our readers' convenience and to adapt the calculations in the Riemannian setting. We will only focus on the case $x \in \partial \Omega$ as the interior case can be found for example in \cite{Scharrer2022}. The key idea is to test \eqref{E:1st-variation-fbv-refined} with suitable test vector fields. In what follows, we denote as in Section \ref{SS:setup} that $r(\cdot)=\operatorname{dist}_g(\cdot,x)$ and $d(\cdot)=\operatorname{dist}_g(\cdot,\partial \Omega)$ be the intrinsic distance function from $x$ and $\partial \Omega$ respectively. Recall the cutoff function $\chi$ defined in section \ref{SS:cutoff}.

Fix $x\in\partial\Omega\cap U$ and let $\rho$ be as in the lemma, so that $\overline{B_\rho(x)}\subset U$. Set
\[
I(\rho):=\int\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)\,d\|V\|,
\qquad
I'(\rho)=\int-\frac r{\rho^2}\chi'\left(\frac r\rho\right)\,d\|V\|\geq0,
\]
and let
\[
A:=\Lambda+(n-1)(\kappa_0+\sqrt{k_0}),\qquad
B:=\Lambda+(n-1)(\kappa_0+C_1),
\]
where $C_1=C_1(n,k_0,\kappa_0)$ is as in \eqref{E:dist-estimates}. First consider
\[
X=\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)r\nabla r\in\mathfrak{X}_c(U).
\]
By Hessian comparison (c.f. \cite[(2.1)]{LiPariseSarnataro2023}),
\[
\left|\operatorname{div}_S X-(n-1)\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)
-\frac r\rho\chi'\left(\frac r\rho\right)|S(\nabla r)|^2\right|
\leq(n-1)\sqrt{k_0}\,r\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right).
\]
Thus \eqref{E:1st-variation-fbv-refined}, \eqref{E:allard-estimate}, and $|H_{\partial\Omega}|\leq(n-1)\kappa_0$ give
\begin{equation}\label{E:monotonicity-proof-1}
\begin{split}
&\int\left((n-1)\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)
+\frac r\rho\chi'\left(\frac r\rho\right)|S(\nabla r)|^2\right)\,dV(y,S)\\
&\hspace{2cm}\leq A\rho I(\rho)
+\frac{\kappa_0}{2}\int r^2\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)\,d\|\delta V\|_{sing}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
To estimate the boundary term while retaining its quadratic factor, use
\[
\tilde X=-r^2\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)\nabla d\in\mathfrak{X}_c(U).
\]
Since $-\nabla d=\nu$ on $\partial\Omega$, and the support of $V$ lies in $\overline\Omega$, the boundary term in \eqref{E:1st-variation-fbv-refined} is now exactly $\int r^2\chi(r/\rho)\,d\|\delta V\|_{sing}$. Moreover, \eqref{E:dist-estimates} gives
\[
|\operatorname{div}_S\tilde X|\leq(n-1)C_1r^2\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)
+2r\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)
-\frac{r^2}{\rho}\chi'\left(\frac r\rho\right).
\]
Consequently,
\begin{align*}
\int r^2\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)\,d\|\delta V\|_{sing}
&\leq\int(Br^2+2r)\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)\,d\|V\|
+\int-\frac{r^2}{\rho}\chi'\left(\frac r\rho\right)\,d\|V\|\\
&\leq(B\rho^2+2\rho)I(\rho)+\rho^2 I'(\rho).
\end{align*}
Here the term $\Lambda$ in $B$ controls $\tilde X\cdot H^{tan}_V$.

Since $\chi'\leq0$ and $|S(\nabla r)|\leq1$, the left-hand side of \eqref{E:monotonicity-proof-1} is at least $(n-1)I(\rho)-\rho I'(\rho)$. Combining the two estimates gives
\[
\rho\left(1+\frac{\kappa_0\rho}{2}\right)I'(\rho)
\geq\left(n-1-(A+\kappa_0)\rho-\frac{\kappa_0B}{2}\rho^2\right)I(\rho).
\]
In particular, \begin{equation}\label{E:monotonicity-diff-inequality}
\frac{d}{d\rho}\left(\frac{I(\rho)}{\rho^{n-1}}\right)
\geq-\frac{A+\frac{n+1}{2}\kappa_0+\frac{\kappa_0B}{2}\rho}
{1+\frac{\kappa_0\rho}{2}}\frac{I(\rho)}{\rho^{n-1}}
\geq-C_5\frac{I(\rho)}{\rho^{n-1}},
\end{equation}
where we may choose any $C_5>0$ with
\[
C_5\geq\max\left\{A+\frac{n+1}{2}\kappa_0,\,B\right\}.
\]
Hence $e^{C_5\rho}I(\rho)/\rho^{n-1}$ is nondecreasing. For centres away from $\partial\Omega$, the radial test field alone gives the interior assertion with the same $C_5$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
\label{R:monotonicity}
We would like to point out that the monotonicity formula above holds only for ambient geodesic balls that are either centred at a boundary point on $\partial \Omega$ or is completely disjoint from the boundary $\partial \Omega$. A standard argument (c.f. \cite[Corollary 17.8]{Simon1983}) then shows that the density function $\Theta_V$ is ``partially'' upper semi-continuous at the boundary in the sense that $\limsup_{x_i \to x} \Theta_V(x_i) \leq \Theta_V(x)$ in general holds for $x \in \partial \Omega$ only when $x_i \in \partial \Omega$ for all $i$. A free boundary half-plane contained in the half-space of $\mathbb{R}^n$ illustrates this point as the boundary points have density $\frac{1}{2}$ and the interior points have density $1$. However, as already observed in the proof of \cite[Theorem 5.7]{LiPariseSarnataro2023}, one can also use the arguments of reflected balls as in \cite[Corollary 3.2]{Grueter1986} (c.f. Appendix \ref{A:reflected-density}) to conclude that the \textit{reflected density} defined by 
\begin{equation}\label{E:reflected-density}
    \overline{\Theta}_V(x):= \left\{\begin{matrix}\begin{aligned}
        &\Theta_V(x) &\text{if }x\notin\partial\Omega \cap U \\ 
        &2\Theta_V(x) &\text{if }x\in\partial\Omega\cap U
        \end{aligned}\end{matrix}\right.
\end{equation}
is actually an upper semi-continuous function in $x$.  In particular, if $V$ is further assumed to be integral, i.e. $V \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(U)$, then we have the following uniform density lower bound\footnote{In fact, $\Theta_V(x) \geq 1$ for all $x \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\| \cap \Omega$. For boundary points $x \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\| \cap \partial \Omega$ which are in the closure of $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \cap \Omega$, it follows from upper semi-continuity of the reflected density. For boundary points $x \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\| \cap \partial \Omega$ which are not in the closure of $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \cap \Omega$, by the free boundary assumption and Lemma \ref{L:constancy lemma De Masi}, $V=\Theta_V |\partial \Omega|$ in a small ball $B_r(x)$ and the conclusion follows once again from integrality of $V$ and upper semi-continuity of the reflected density.}
                                                                               
\begin{equation}
    \label{E:density-lower-bound}
\Theta_V(x) \geq \frac{1}{2} \quad \text{for every $x \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\|$.} 
\end{equation} 
By the monotonicity formula, this implies the following uniform density ratio bounds
\begin{equation}
\label{E:density-ratio-bound}
\frac{1}{C_6} \leq \frac{\|V\|(B_\rho(x))}{\rho^{n-1}} \leq C_6 \frac{\|V\|(B_{R_x}(x))}{R_x^{n-1}} 
\end{equation}
for any $0 < \rho < R_x=\min\{R_6,\operatorname{dist}_g(x,\partial U)\}$ when $x \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\| \cap \partial \Omega$; and $0<\rho < R_x=\min\{R_6,\operatorname{dist}_g(x,\partial \Omega \cup \partial U)\}$ when $x \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\| \setminus \partial \Omega$. Here, $C_6=C_6(n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0,\Lambda)>0$ and $R_6=R_6(n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0)>0$ are positive constants.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- remark-10: body_md -->
\label{R:weighted-monotonicity}
The same test fields give a weighted version of \eqref{E:monotonicity-diff-inequality}. Let $h\in C^1_c(U)$ be nonnegative and set
\[
I_h(\rho):=\int h\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)\,d\|V\|,
\qquad
G_h(\rho):=\int r\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)|S(\nabla h)|\,dV.
\]
At a boundary centre, test \eqref{E:1st-variation-fbv-refined} with $hX$ and $h\tilde X$, where $X$ and $\tilde X$ are the fields in the proof above. The additional derivative terms are bounded by $G_h(\rho)$ and $\rho G_h(\rho)$, respectively. With the same constants $A,B$, this yields
\[
\begin{split}
\rho\left(1+\frac{\kappa_0\rho}{2}\right)I_h'(\rho)
\geq{}&\left(n-1-(A+\kappa_0)\rho-\frac{\kappa_0B}{2}\rho^2\right)I_h(\rho)\\
&-\left(1+\frac{\kappa_0\rho}{2}\right)G_h(\rho).
\end{split}
\]
Thus, for the same choice of $C_5$,
\begin{equation}\label{E:weighted-monotonicity-diff-inequality}
\frac{d}{d\rho}\left(\frac{e^{C_5\rho}}{\rho^{n-1}}
\int h\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)\,d\|V\|\right)
\geq-\frac{e^{C_5\rho}}{\rho^n}\int r\chi\left(\frac r\rho\right)|S(\nabla h)|\,dV.
\end{equation}
At an interior centre the same inequality follows using $hX$ alone. Integrating in $\rho$ and then letting the cutoffs increase to the characteristic function of $[0,1)$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{E:weighted-montonicity}
\begin{split}
&\frac{e^{C_5R_2}}{R_2^{n-1}}\int_{B_{R_2}(x)}h\,d\|V\|
-\frac{e^{C_5R_1}}{R_1^{n-1}}\int_{B_{R_1}(x)}h\,d\|V\|\\
&\qquad\geq-\int_{R_1}^{R_2}\frac{e^{C_5t}}{t^n}
\left(\int_{G(B_t(x))}r\,|S(\nabla h)|\,dV\right)dt
\end{split}
\end{equation}
for $0<R_1<R_2$ in the corresponding range of Lemma \ref{L:monotonicity limit varifold}.
<!-- end remark-10 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{P:tangent cones FB stationary varifolds}
    Let $V \in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(U)$ be a varifold with free boundary in $\partial \Omega \cap U$, $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \subset \overline{\Omega} \cap U$ and $\|H^{tan}_V\|_{L^\infty(U)} \leq \Lambda$. Then for any $x\in \operatorname{spt}\|V\|\cap \partial\Omega$, the space $\operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$ of varifold tangents at $x$ is non-empty and compact (in the weak topology). Moreover, for any $\mathbf{C} \in\operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$ the following holds:
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item $\mathbf{C}$ is free boundary stationary with free boundary on $T_x \partial \Omega$ and $\operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}\| \subset T_x^{+} \widetilde{M}$, i.e.
        \[ \delta \mathbf{C} (X)=0 \quad \text{for all $X \in \mathfrak{X}_c(T_x \widetilde{M})$ such that $X \cdot \nu(x)=0$ along $T_x \partial \Omega$};\]
        \item $\Theta_\mathbf{C}(0)=\Theta_V(x)$ and $\|\mathbf{C}\|$ is a cone with vertex at the origin of $T_x \widetilde{M}$, i.e. 
        \[ (\eta_{0,r})_* \|\mathbf{C}\|= r^{n-1}\|\mathbf{C}\| \quad \text{for all $r>0$}; \] 
        \item The generalized boundary measure $\|\delta \mathbf{C}\|_{sing}$ in $T_x \widetilde{M}$ is scale-invariant (with dimension $n-2$), i.e. 
        \[ (\eta_{0,r})_* \|\delta \mathbf{C}\|_{sing}=r^{n-2} \|\delta \mathbf{C}\|_{sing} \quad \text{for all $r>0$.}\]
        Here, we identify $T_x \widetilde{M}$ with $\mathbb{R}^n$ and denote $T_x^+ \widetilde{M}:=\{v \in T_x \widetilde{M}: v \cdot \nu(x) \leq 0\}$ the tangent half-space that points into $\Omega$, and the homothety maps $\eta_{p,r}:T_x \widetilde{M} \to T_x \widetilde{M}$ defined by $\eta_{p,r}(q):=r^{-1}(q-p)$ (c.f. Section \ref{SS:Euclidean}).
    \end{enumerate}
    
    \noindent If we assume, furthermore, that $V$ is rectifiable, i.e. $V \in \mathbf{RV}_{n-1}(U)$, and the density of $V$ satisfies a uniform positive lower bound, i.e.
    \begin{equation}
    \label{E:uniform-lower-bound}
    \Theta_V(x) \geq 1 \text{ for $\|V\|$-a.e. $x\in U$.}
    \end{equation}
    then for any $\mathbf{C} \in\operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$, we have $\mathbf{C} \in \mathbf{RV}_{n-1}(T_x \widetilde{M})$ is a varifold cone with vertex at the origin of $T_x \widetilde{M}$, i.e. 
        \[ (\eta_{0,r})_\sharp \mathbf{C}= \mathbf{C} \quad \text{for all $r>0$}.\]
        If $V \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(U)$ (in which case \eqref{E:uniform-lower-bound} is trivially satisfied), then $C \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(T_x \widetilde{M})$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
We provide again the key steps of the proof of \cite{Masi2020} adapted to the Riemannian setting. Consider a sequence of positive numbers $r_i \to 0$, and the sequence of varifolds\footnote{More precisely, for any fixed $R>0$, $V_i$ is an integral varifold in $B^\delta_R(0)$ for all sufficiently large $i$.} 
\[ V_i:=(\eta_{0,r_i} \circ \exp_x^{-1})_\sharp V \in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(T_x \widetilde{M}).\]
Since we are working with geodesic normal coordinates of $(\widetilde{M},g)$ centred at $x$, we have the standard estimates from \eqref{E:inj-bound} and \eqref{E:sec-bound}
\begin{equation}
\label{E:metric-estimate}
|g_{ij}(q)-\delta_{ij}| \leq C(n,k_0) |q|^2
\end{equation}
for sufficiently small $|q|$ depending only on $n,k_0$ and $i_0$. This implies the following estimate on the exponential map and its Jacobian factor (recall \cite[(39.1)]{Simon1983}):
\begin{equation}
    \label{E:dexp-estimate}
 \max\{\|d(\exp^{-1}_x)|_y\|,\|d(\exp_x)|_{\exp_x^{-1}(y)}\| \} \leq 1+ C(n,k_0) \operatorname{dist}_g(y,x),
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
    \label{E:Jacobian-estimate}
 |J_S (\exp^{-1}_x)(y)-1| \leq C(n,k_0) \operatorname{dist}_g(y,x),
\end{equation}
From \eqref{E:dexp-estimate} and \eqref{E:Jacobian-estimate}, by the monotonicity formula Lemma \ref{L:monotonicity limit varifold} and the fact that the rescaled pushforward metrics $r_i^{-2}(\exp_x^{-1})_* g$ converges in $C^2_{loc}$ to the flat Euclidean metric $\delta$ on $T_x \widetilde{M}$, $V_i$ has locally uniformly bounded mass in $T_x \widetilde{M}$ as varifolds in $(T_x \widetilde{M},\delta)$. Therefore, standard compactness theorem for Radon measures implies that, after passing to a subsequence, we have $V_i$ converging weakly to some $\mathbf{C} \in \operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$. This implies $\operatorname{VarTan}(V,x) \neq \emptyset$ and it follows from the same argument and the definition that $\operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$ is compact in the weak topology. 

Suppose now $\mathbf{C}=\lim_{i\to \infty} V_i \in \operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$. We now compute its first variation as varifolds in Euclidean space $(T_x \widetilde{M},\delta)$ using \eqref{E:1st-variation} as follows: suppose $X \in \mathfrak{X}_c(T_x \widetilde{M})$ with $\operatorname{spt} X \subset B^\delta_R(0)$, then for all sufficiently large $i$ we have
\[ \delta V_i(X)=\int_{G(B^{\delta}_R(0))} (\operatorname{div}_T^{\delta} X)(p) \; dV_i(p,T)= \frac{1}{r_i^{n-1}} \int_{G(B^{\delta}_{Rr_i}(0))} (\operatorname{div}^{\delta}_T X)(r_i^{-1} q) \; d \overline{V} (q,T) \]
\[ = \frac{1}{r_i^{n-2}} \int_{G(B^{\delta}_{Rr_i}(0))} \operatorname{div}^{g}_T \left( X(r_i^{-1} q) \right) \; d \overline{V} (q,T) - \frac{1}{r_i^{n-2}} \int_{G(B^{\delta}_{Rr_i}(0))} (\operatorname{div}^{g}_T -\operatorname{div}^{\delta}_T) \left( X(r_i^{-1} q) \right)  \; d \overline{V} (q,T)  \]
where $\overline{V}:=(\exp^{-1}_x)_\sharp V \in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(T_x \widetilde{M})$\footnote{Again, $\overline{V}$ is more precisely a varifold in some open neighbourhood of $0$.}. By the definition of pushforward of varifolds, we have $d \overline{V} (q,T)=J_S (\exp^{-1}_x)(y)\; d V (y,S)$ where $q=\exp^{-1}_x(y)$, $T=(d \exp^{-1}_x)|_y(S)$, and $J_S (\exp^{-1}_x)(y):=\sqrt{\det\left((d\exp^{-1}_x|_S)^* \circ (d\exp^{-1}_x|_S) \right)}(y)$ is the Jacobian factor of $\exp_x^{-1}$ relative to $S$. It then follows from \eqref{E:metric-estimate} that for all $i$ sufficiently large
\[ \left|\operatorname{div}^{g}_T \left( X(r_i^{-1} q) \right) \right| \leq 2 \left( |q| |X(r_i^{-1}q)|+ r_i^{-1} |DX(r_i^{-1}q)| \right), \]
\[ \left|(\operatorname{div}^{g}_T -\operatorname{div}^{\delta}_T) \left( X(r_i^{-1} q) \right) \right| \leq C(n,k_0) \left( |q| |X(r_i^{-1}q)|+|q|^2 r_i^{-1} |DX(r_i^{-1}q)| \right). \]
Combining these estimate with \eqref{E:Jacobian-estimate}, we see that
\begin{equation}
\label{E:proof of tangent cones FB stationary varifolds}
|\delta V_i(X) - r_i^{-(n-2)} \delta V(X_i)| \leq C(n,k_0) r_i \int \left( r_i |p| |X(p)|+|p| |DX(p)| \right) \; d\|V_i\|(p) 
\end{equation} 
where $X_i:=d(\exp_x)(X \circ \eta_{0,r_i}) \in \mathfrak{X}_c(U)$ is supported inside $B_{R r_i}(x)=\exp_x(B^\delta_{Rr_i}(0))$ for all large $i$. 
                                                                                                                                                                                          
Note that $V_i$ may not have locally bounded first variation in $T_x \widetilde{M}$ due to the term involving $DX$. On the other hand, using \eqref{E:1st-variation-fbv-refined}, we have
\[ r_i^{-(n-2)} \delta V(X_i)= - \frac{1}{r_i^{n-1}} \int_{\partial \Omega} X_i \cdot (r_i H^{tan}_V+r_i H_{\partial \Omega}) \; d\|V\| + \frac{1}{r_i^{n-2}} \int_{\partial \Omega} X_i \cdot \nu \; d\|\delta V\|_{sing}. \]
By \eqref{E:ball-curvatures} and \eqref{E:Jacobian-estimate}, we have for all sufficiently large $i$ the following estimate for the first term on the right hand side above:
\[\left| \frac{1}{r_i^{n-1}} \int_{\partial \Omega} X_i \cdot (r_i H^{tan}_V+r_i H_{\partial \Omega}) \; d\|V\| \right| \leq C(n,\kappa_0,\Lambda) r_i \int |X(p)| \; d\|V_i\|(p). \]
For the second term, if we further assume that $X$ is tangential to $T_x \partial \Omega$, by \eqref{E:metric-estimate} and \eqref{E:ball-curvatures}, we have the estimate
\[\|X_i \cdot \nu\|_{L^\infty(B_{Rr_i}(x))} \leq C(\kappa_0) R r_i\left( R \|DX\|_{L^\infty(U_{C(\kappa_0) R^2 r_i}(T_x\partial \Omega))} + \|X\|_{L^\infty(U_{C(\kappa_0) R^2 r_i}(T_x\partial \Omega))} \right)  \]
where $U_h(T_x \partial \Omega):=\{p \in T_x \widetilde{M}: \operatorname{dist}^\delta(p,T_x \partial \Omega) < h\}$ denotes the parallel slab of width $h$ around the hyperplane $T_x \partial \Omega$. Combining this with \eqref{E:local-volume-bound-fbv-stationary}, we can estimate the second term as follow:
\[ \left| \frac{1}{r_i^{n-2}} \int_{\partial \Omega} X_i \cdot \nu \; d\|\delta V\|_{sing} \right| \leq C(n,k_0,\kappa_0,\Theta_V(x)) R^{n-1} r_i  \left( R \|DX\|_{L^\infty} + \|X\|_{L^\infty} \right).  \]
Putting all these estimates together, \eqref{E:proof of tangent cones FB stationary varifolds} can be written as: for all sufficiently large $i$, and $X \in \mathfrak{X}_c(T_x \widetilde{M})$ with $X$ is tangential to $T_x \partial \Omega$, $\operatorname{spt} X \subset B^\delta_R(0)$, we have
\[ |\delta V_i(X)| \leq C(n,k_0,\kappa_0,\Lambda) r_i \int \left( (1+r_i |p|) |X(p)|+|p| |DX(p)| \right) \; d\|V_i\|(p) \]
\begin{equation}
\label{E:proof of tangent cones FB stationary varifolds-2}
\hspace{2cm} +  C(n,k_0,\kappa_0,\Theta_V(x)) R^{n-1} r_i \left( R \|DX\|_{L^\infty} + \|X\|_{L^\infty} \right)
\end{equation} 
where the $L^\infty$ norms on the right hand side are taken in the slab $U_{C(\kappa_0) R^2 r_i}(T_x\partial \Omega)$ as before.

The estimate \eqref{E:proof of tangent cones FB stationary varifolds-2} on the first variation of $V_i$ implies that 
\begin{equation}
\label{E:limit-stationary-Vi}
\lim_{i\to \infty} \delta V_i(X)=0 \quad \text{$\forall X \in \mathfrak{X}_c(T_x \widetilde{M})$ tangential to $T_x \partial \Omega$.} 
\end{equation}
By the continuity of first variation with respect to varifold convergence, we conclude that $\mathbf{C}$ is free boundary stationary with free boundary on $T_x \partial \Omega$. Moreover, as $\operatorname{spt} \|\overline{V}\| \subset \exp_x^{-1}(\overline{\Omega})$ and $\eta_{0,r_i}(\exp^{-1}_x(\Omega))$ converges locally in Hausdorff topology to $T^+_x \widetilde{M}$, it is clear that $\operatorname{spt} \|C\| \subset T^+_x \widetilde{M}$. 

Next, we move on to the assertions in (ii) and (iii). Because of \eqref{E:Jacobian-estimate}, $\Theta_{\overline{V}}(0)$ exists and is equal to $\Theta_{V}(x)$. As $\mathbf{C} \in \operatorname{VarTan}(\overline{V},0)$, standard arguments (c.f. \cite[Section 42]{Simon1983}) shows that 
\[ \frac{\|\mathbf{C}\|(B_R(0))}{\omega_{n-1} R^{n-1}}  \equiv \Theta_V(x) \quad \text{for all $R>0$}.\]
Recall that $\mathbf{C}$ is free boundary stationary in $T_x \widetilde{M}$ with free boundary on the hyperplane $T_x \partial \Omega$, we therefore conclude from the monotonicity formula that $\|\mathbf{C}\|$ is a cone centred at $0$. This proves (ii). Finally, (iii) is a direct consequence of (i) and (ii) because of the scaling of first variation \cite[Remark 4.12(1)]{Allard1972} and $\|\delta \mathbf{C}\|=\|\delta \mathbf{C}\|_{sing}$.

To establish the other assertions in the proposition, it remains to show that $\mathbf{C}$ is rectifiable (resp. integral) under the assumption that $V$ is rectifiable (resp. integral) satisfying \eqref{E:uniform-lower-bound}. First of all, notice that we cannot apply the standard compactness theorem for rectifiable or integral varifolds as $V_i$ does not have locally bounded variation due to the terms involving $DX$ on the right hand side of \eqref{E:proof of tangent cones FB stationary varifolds-2}. To get around this difficulty, we first note that since $V \in \mathbf{RV}_{n-1}(U)$ is free boundary stationary with uniform density lower bound \eqref{E:uniform-lower-bound}, by Remark \ref{R:monotonicity} and \eqref{E:density-ratio-bound} therein, we have the following uniform density ratio bounds at the geometric level:
\[ \frac{1}{C_6} \leq \frac{\|V\|(B_\rho(y))}{\rho^{n-1}} \leq C_6 \frac{\|V\|(B_{R_y}(y))}{R_y^{n-1}} \]
for all $y \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\| \cap B_{d_x/2}(x)$ provided that $0 < \rho < R_y:=\min\{R_6,d_x/2\}$ when $y \in \partial \Omega$; and $0<\rho< R_y:=\min\{R_6,d_x/2,\operatorname{dist}_g(y,\partial \Omega)\}$ when $y \in \Omega$. Here, $d_x:=\operatorname{dist}_g(x,\partial U)>0$. It is important to keep in mind that $C_6, R_6$ are positive constants depending only on the ambient geometric quantities $n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0$ and $\Lambda$ but not on $V$ and $y$. These estimates pass to the level of geodesic normal coordinates (due to \eqref{E:dexp-estimate}, \eqref{E:Jacobian-estimate} and the fact that uniform density ratio bounds are bi-Lipschitz invariant) to yield
                                                                                                                                                
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      
for any fixed $R>0$ and all sufficiently large $i$,
\begin{equation}
    \label{E:density-ratio-bound-Vi}
\frac{1}{2C_6} \leq \frac{\|V_i\|(B^\delta_\rho(p_i))}{\rho^{n-1}} \leq 2C_6 \frac{\|V_i\|(B^\delta_{R_{p_i}}(p_i))}{R_{p_i}^{n-1}}
\end{equation} 
for all $p_i \in \operatorname{spt} \|V_i\| \cap B^\delta_R(0)$ provided that $0 < \rho < R_{p_i}:=R$ when $p_i \in \eta_{0,r_i}(\exp_x^{-1}(\partial \Omega))$; and $0<\rho< R_{p_i}:=\min\{R, \operatorname{dist}_\delta (p_i,\eta_{0,r_i}(\exp_x^{-1}(\partial \Omega)))\}$ when $p_i \in \eta_{0,r_i}(\exp_x^{-1}\Omega)$. Recall the elementary observation that density is preserved under $C^1$-diffeomorphism, we have $\Theta_{V_i}$ exists everywhere in $B^\delta_R(0)$ and $\Theta_{V_i}(p_i) \geq \frac{1}{2}$ for every $p_i \in \operatorname{spt}\|V_i\| \cap B^\delta_R(0)$ because of \eqref{E:density-lower-bound}. 

We now argue that the uniform density ratio bounds (with slightly different constants) pass on to the tangent varifold $\mathbf{C}=\lim_{i \to \infty} \|V_i\|$. Let $p \in \operatorname{spt} \|C\|$, it is clear from definition that there exists $p_i \in \operatorname{spt} \|V_i\|$ such that $p_i \to p$. Since $\operatorname{spt} \|C\| \subset T^+_x \widetilde{M}$, either $p$ is an interior point, i.e. $p \notin T_x \partial \Omega$, or $p$ is a boundary point, i.e. $p \in T_x \partial \Omega$. For an interior point $p \notin T_x \partial \Omega$, the approximating sequence $p_i \in \operatorname{spt}\|V_i\|$ will also be interior points for all sufficiently large $i$. Applying the interior case of \eqref{E:density-ratio-bound-Vi}, we have for such $p$ the following density ratio lower bound 
\[ \frac{\|\mathbf{C}\|(B_\rho^\delta(p))}{\rho^{n-1}} \geq \frac{1}{4^n C_6}>0\]
provided that $0< \rho < \min\{R, \frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}_\delta(p,T_x \partial \Omega)\}$. Since $\mathbf{C}$ is free boundary stationary, the density $\Theta_{\mathbf{C}}$ exists everywhere and letting $\rho \to 0$ in the above inequality implies that $\Theta_{\mathbf{C}}(p) \geq \frac{1}{4^n \omega_{n-1} C_6}>0$ for all interior points $p \in \operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}\| \setminus T_x \partial \Omega$. Suppose now $p \in \operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}\| \cap T_x \partial \Omega$ is a boundary point. If there are infinitely many $p_i$ in the approximating sequence that lies on the boundary $\eta_{0,r_i}(\exp_x^{-1}\Omega)$, then the same argument as above gives the same density lower bound for such $p$. It remains to treat the remaining case for those $p \in \operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}\| \cap T_x \partial \Omega$ but there exists some $r>0$ with $\operatorname{spt}\|V_i\| \cap B^\delta_r(p)$ contained in the interior $\exp_x^{-1}(\Omega)$ for all sufficiently large $i$. Going back to the geometric setting, it means that $V$ indeed has empty free boundary and hence we are once again reduced to the interior situation which has been treated before\footnote{More precisely, in this case although $\operatorname{dist}_\delta(p_i,\eta_{0,r_i}(\exp_x^{-1}\Omega)) \to 0$, we can still obtain \eqref{E:density-ratio-bound-Vi} with $R_{p_i}$ replaced by a fixed $R_0>0$ independent of $i$.}. Finally, since $\mathbf{C} \in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(T_x \widetilde{M})$ is free boundary stationary with free boundary in $T_x \partial \Omega$ (hence $\mathbf{C}$ has locally bounded first variation in $T_x \widetilde{M}$) and its density exists and has a fixed positive lower bound everywhere in $\operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}\|$, we can apply Allard's Rectifiability Theorem \cite[5.5(1)]{Allard1972} to conclude that $\mathbf{C} \in \mathbf{RV}_{n-1}(T_x \widetilde{M})$. Once we know that $\mathbf{C}$ is rectifiable and is free boundary stationary in $T_x \widetilde{M}$ with free boundary on the hyperplane $T_x \partial \Omega$, standard arguments (c.f. \cite[Section 42]{Simon1983}) show that $\mathbf{C}$ is a varifold cone centred at $0$. 

We now show that $\mathbf{C}$ is integral, i.e. $\mathbf{C} \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(T_x \widetilde{M})$, if $V$ is integral. In other words, we need to show that $\Theta_{\mathbf{C}}(p) \in \mathbb{N}$ for $\|\mathbf{C}\|$-a.e. $p \in \operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}\|$. First we address the interior points, i.e. $p \in \operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}\| \setminus T_x \partial \Omega$. Since we already know that $\mathbf{C}$ is rectifiable, we can assume without loss of generality that $\|\mathbf{C}\|$ has an approximate tangent space $T_p\mathbf{C} \in \mathbf{G}_x(n,n-1)$ at $p$ in the sense that
\[ \lim_{s \to 0} s^{-(n-1)}(\eta_{p,s})_\sharp \|\mathbf{C}\| = \Theta_{\mathbf{C}}(p) \mathcal{H}^{n-1} \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} T_p \mathbf{C}   \]
as Radon measures in $T_x \widetilde{M}$. Our goal is to show that $\Theta_{\mathbf{C}}(p) \in \mathbb{N}$. 

Since $\lim_{i \to \infty} V_i = \mathbf{C}$ as varifolds, we also have $\lim_{i \to \infty} \|V_i\|= \|\mathbf{C}\|$ and therefore we can fix some sequence $s_i \to 0$ and then, after passing to a subsequence, assume that 
\begin{equation}
    \label{E:proof-tangent-cone-1}
\lim_{i \to \infty} r_i s_i^{-1} =0 \qquad \text{and} \qquad \lim_{i \to \infty} (\eta_{p,s_i})_\sharp V_i = \Theta_{\mathbf{C}}(p) |T_p \mathbf{C}|
\end{equation} 
as varifolds in $T_x \widetilde{M}$. Again, although each $W_i:=(\eta_{p,s_i})_\sharp V_i \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(T_x \widetilde{M})$, we cannot apply the compactness theorem for integral varifolds in \cite[Theorem 6.4]{Allard1972} to conclude that the limit is integral since $(\eta_{p,s_i})_\sharp V_i$ a-priori do not have locally bounded first variation. Instead, we will proceed as in the proof of \cite[Theorem 6.4]{Allard1972}. Since all the $W_i$ satisfy uniform density ratio bounds as in \eqref{E:density-ratio-bound-Vi}, we have $\operatorname{spt} \|W_i\|$ converges to $T_p \mathbf{C}$ locally in Hausdorff topology (c.f. \cite[Remark 10]{SchoenSimon81}). Let $\pi:T_x \widetilde{M} \to T_x \widetilde{M}$ denote the orthogonal projection onto $T_p \mathbf{C}$. Then, we have from \eqref{E:proof-tangent-cone-1} and the rectifiability of $W_i$ that after projecting $W_i$ inside the slab $U_\epsilon(T_p \mathbf{C}):=\{q \in T_x \widetilde{M} : |q-\pi(q)|<\epsilon\}$ of width $\epsilon >0$ (to be fixed later) we have 
\[ \lim_{i \to \infty} \pi_\sharp (W_i \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} G(U_\epsilon(T_p \mathbf{C}))) = \Theta_{\mathbf{C}}(p) |T_p \mathbf{C}|.\] 
Since $W_i \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(T_x \widetilde{M})$, after possibly re-defining on a set of $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}$-measure zero, we consider the $L^1_{loc}$ function $\psi^\epsilon_i:T_p \mathbf{C} \to \mathbb{N} \cup \{\infty\}$ defined by
\[ \psi^\epsilon_i(q)=\sum_{\substack{\tilde{q} \in \operatorname{spt} \|W_i\| \cap U_\epsilon(T_p \mathbf{C}) \\
\pi(\tilde{q})=q}} \Theta_{W_i}(\tilde{q})  \]
converges weakly in $L^1_{loc}(T_p \mathbf{C};\mathcal{H}^{n-1})$ to the constant function $\Theta_\mathbf{C}(p)$. However, weak $L^1$-convergence is not sufficient to conclude convergence almost everywhere. We need to upgrade it (after suitable truncation) to strong $L^1$-convergence. This will be achieved by the following (c.f. \cite[Lemma 42.9]{Simon1983})

\vspace{1ex}

\textit{Claim: For any $\gamma \in (0,1)$, there exists $\epsilon=\epsilon(\gamma)>0$ such that for any sufficiently large $i$, there exists $A_i \subset B^\delta_1(0) \cap T_p \mathbf{C}$ with $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(A_i)<\gamma$ such that for every $q \in (B^\delta_1(0) \cap T_p \mathbf{C}) \setminus A_i$, we have}
\begin{equation}
\label{E:sheeting-estimates}
\psi^\epsilon_i(q) \leq  (1+\gamma) \frac{\|W_i\|(B^\delta_2(q))}{\omega_{n-1} 2^n} + \gamma.
\end{equation}

\vspace{1ex}

We first see how the claim implies $\Theta_\mathbf{C}(p) \in \mathbb{N}$. Choose any sequence $\gamma_i \to 0$, there exists $\epsilon_i \to 0$ and, after passing to a subsequence, there exists $A_i \subset B^\delta_1(0) \cap T_p \mathbf{C}$ with $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(A_i) \to 0$ such that by \eqref{E:proof-tangent-cone-1}
\[ \psi^{\epsilon_i}_i(q) \leq \Theta_\mathbf{C}(p) + \frac{1}{i} \quad \text{for all $q \in (B^\delta_1(0) \cap T_p \mathbf{C}) \setminus A_i$.}\]
Hence, for any positive integer $N>\Theta_\mathbf{C}(p)$, the integer-valued function $\max\{\psi^{\epsilon_i}_i,N\}$ converges strongly in $L^1(B^\delta_1(0) \cap T_p \mathbf{C};\mathcal{H}^{n-1})$ to $\Theta_\mathbf{C}(p)$. By taking a subsequence that converges $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}$-a.e. in $B^\delta_1(0) \cap T_p \mathbf{C}$, we conclude that $\Theta_\mathbf{C}(p) \in \mathbb{N}$. 

It remains to establish the claim above. Fix $\gamma \in (0,1)$. We will follow the arguments in \cite[Lemma 42.9]{Simon1983} with the caveat that $W_i$ does not have locally bounded first variation. First of all, we observe that by \eqref{E:proof-tangent-cone-1}, for all sufficiently large $i$ we have 
\begin{equation}
\label{E:Wi-uniform-mass-bound}
\|W_i\|(B^\delta_4(0)) \leq 5^{n-1} \omega_{n-1} \Theta_{\mathbf{C}}(p).
\end{equation}
Therefore, $W_i$ have uniformly bounded mass in $B^\delta_4(0)$.                                  
                                                                                                                                                     
Moreover, \eqref{E:proof-tangent-cone-1} also implies the $L^1$-tilt excess for $W_i$ in $B^\delta_4(0)$ converges to zero, i.e.
\[ \lim_{i \to \infty} \int_{G(B^\delta_4(0))} \|S-T_p \mathbf{C}\| \; dW_i(q,S) = 0. \]
For any $\tau>0$, we now define the subset $A^\tau_i:=\pi(\tilde{A}^\tau_i) \subset B^\delta_1(0) \cap T_p \mathbf{C}$ where
\[ \tilde{A}^\tau_i:=\left\{ \tilde{q} \in \operatorname{spt}\|W_i\| \cap B^\delta_2(0) \; : \; \exists \rho \in (0,2) \text{ such that } \int_{G(B^\delta_{\rho}(q))} \|S-T_p \mathbf{C}\| \; dW_i(\tilde{q},S) >  \tau \rho^{n-1}  \right\}. \]
Since $W_i$ satisfies uniform density ratio bounds, by Besicovitch covering lemma \cite[Lemma 4.6]{Simon1983}, we have the following estimate on the measure of $\tilde{A}^\tau_i$:
\[ \|W_i\|(\tilde{A}^\tau_i) \leq C(n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0,\Lambda) \tau^{-1}  \int_{G(B^\delta_4(0))} \|S-T_p \mathbf{C}\| \; dW_i(q,S).\]
Since $W_i \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(T_x \widetilde{M})$, this implies 
\[ \lim_{i \to \infty} \mathcal{H}^{n-1}(A^\tau_i) =0 \quad \text{for any fixed $\tau>0$.} \] 

Next, by the (interior) weighted monotonicity formula \eqref{E:weighted-montonicity} applied to the original free boundary varifold $V \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(U)$, together with \eqref{E:dexp-estimate}, \eqref{E:Jacobian-estimate} and some scaling arguments as before, we see that for all sufficiently large $i$, and any $q \in B^\delta_1(0) \setminus \tilde{A}^\tau_i$ (note that $q$ need not be in $\operatorname{spt} \|W_i\|$) and any $h>0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{E:slabbed-ball-estimate-1}    
\frac{\|W_i\|(U^h_{R_1}(q))}{\omega_{n-1} R_1^{n-1}} \leq (1+C(n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0,\Lambda) r_i s_iR_2) \frac{\|W_i\|(U^{2h}_{R_2}(q))}{\omega_{n-1} R_2^{n-1}} + \frac{4}{3} \tau \left(\frac{R_2}{h}\right)^{n-1}
\end{equation} 
for any $0<R_1 \leq R_2\leq 2$. Here, we denote $U^h_R(q):= B^\delta_R(q) \cap \{p \in T_x \widetilde{M}: |N_{T_p \mathbf{C}} \cdot (p-q)| < h\}$ where $N_{T_p \mathbf{C}}$ is a unit vector orthogonal to $T_p \mathbf{C}$. If furthermore $q \in \operatorname{spt} \|W_i\|$, by letting $R_1 \to 0$ and $R_2=h=2$ in \eqref{E:slabbed-ball-estimate-1}, we obtain (note that $U^{2h}_2(q) \subset B^\delta_2(q)$ for any $h>0$)
\[ \Theta_{W_i}(q) \leq (1+\gamma) \frac{\|W_i\|(B_2^\delta(q))}{\omega_{n-1} 2^{n-1}} + \gamma\]
for all sufficiently large $i$ and sufficiently small $\tau$ (both depending on $\gamma$ besides the ambient constants $n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0$ and $\Lambda$ but independent of $q$). This proves the claim in case there is only one $\tilde{q}\in \operatorname{spt} \|W_i\| \cap U_\epsilon(T_p \mathbf{C})$ such that $\pi(\tilde{q})=q \in (B^\delta_1(0) \cap T_p \mathbf{C}) \setminus A_i$ (note that all such $\tilde{q} \in B^\delta_2(0)$) with $A_i=A_i^\tau$. 

We will finish the proof by inducting on the number of $\tilde{q}$'s in $\operatorname{spt} \|W_i\| \cap U_\epsilon(T_p \mathbf{C})$ such that $\pi(\tilde{q})=q \in (B^\delta_1(0) \cap T_p \mathbf{C}) \setminus A^\tau_i$. Suppose there are $N$ distinct $\tilde{q}_1,\cdots,\tilde{q}_N \in \operatorname{spt}\|W_i\| \cap U_\epsilon(T_p \mathbf{C})$ such that $\pi(\tilde{q}_j)=q \in (B^\delta_1(0) \cap T_p \mathbf{C}) \setminus A^\tau_i$ for $j=1,\cdots,N$. We assert that for all sufficiently large $i$ (depending on $\tau$ and $n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0,\Lambda$), with $\epsilon=\tau^{\frac{1}{2(n-1)}}/2$ for any $\tau \in (0,1)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{E:multi-layer-monotonicity}
\sum_{j=1}^N \frac{\|W_i\|(U^h_{R}(\tilde{q}_j))}{\omega_{n-1} R^{n-1}} \leq (1+ C(n,N) \tau^{\frac{1}{2(n-1)}} ) \frac{\|W_i\|(B_2^\delta(0))}{\omega_{n-1} 2^{n-1}} + C(n,N) \tau^{1/2}
\end{equation}
provided that $h \leq \frac{1}{4}\min_{j \neq k} |\tilde{q}_j - \tilde{q}_k|$ and $R< \min\{2,\tau^{-\frac{1}{2(n-1)}} C(N) h\}$.  

To establish \eqref{E:multi-layer-monotonicity}, we first note that by the interior monotonicity formula (Lemma \ref{L:monotonicity limit varifold}), the same argument as before shows for any $q \in B^\delta_1(0)$ (again not necessarily inside $\operatorname{spt} \|W_i\|$), $0 <R_1 \leq R_2 \leq 2$, and all sufficiently large $i$,
\begin{equation}
\label{E:multi-layer-monotonicity-1}
   \frac{\|W_i\|(B^\delta_{R_1}(q))}{\omega_{n-1} R_1^{n-1}} \leq (1+ C(n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0,\Lambda) r_i s_iR_2) \frac{\|W_i\|(B^\delta_{R_2}(q))}{\omega_{n-1} R_2^{n-1}}  
\end{equation}
                                                                                                                  
                 
                                  
                                                                                                                                                          
               
For the purpose of induction on $N$ in \eqref{E:multi-layer-monotonicity}, note that when $N=1$, it follows directly from \eqref{E:slabbed-ball-estimate-1} by setting $R_1=R_2=R$, $C(1)=2$, $\epsilon=1$ and using \eqref{E:multi-layer-monotonicity-1}. Suppose now \eqref{E:multi-layer-monotonicity} holds up to $N-1$. Consider now $N$ distinct $\tilde{q}_1,\cdots,\tilde{q}_N \in \operatorname{spt}\|W_i\| \cap U_\epsilon(T_p \mathbf{C})$ such that $\pi(\tilde{q}_j)=q \in (B^\delta_1(0) \cap T_p \mathbf{C}) \setminus A^\tau_i$ for $j=1,\cdots,N$. Set 
\[ \hat{h}:= \min_{1 \leq j \neq k \leq N} |\tilde{q}_j - \tilde{q}_k| \leq \frac{2\epsilon}{N-1}.\]
On the other hand, it is elementary to see that, after possibly re-labelling again, we can assume that\footnote{This is based on the elementary fact: there exists constants $C(N)>0$ such that given any collection $\{a_1,\cdots,a_N\}$ of $N$ points in $\mathbb{R}$ with $d:=\min_{1 \leq j \neq k \neq N} |a_j - a_k|$ (by convention, we take $d=+\infty$ if $N=1)$, there is always a proper sub-collection $\{\hat{a}_1,\cdots,\hat{a}_K\}$ with $1 \leq K \leq N-1$ and $\hat{d} \geq \frac{3d}{2}$ such that $\hat{d} \leq C(N) d$, $4\hat{d} \leq \min_{1 \leq j \neq k \neq K} |\hat{a}_j - \hat{a}_k|$
\[ \bigcup_{j=1}^N \left(a_j-\frac{d}{2},a_j+\frac{d}{2} \right) \subset \bigcup_{i=1}^K \left(\hat{a}_i-\hat{d},\hat{a}_i+\hat{d} \right).  \]
This can be proved by a simple (but tedious) induction argument on $N$. The basic idea is to analyse different cases corresponding to the relative distance between adjacent points and throwing away (in finitely many steps bounded by $N$) those within distance of order $d$ to increase the mutual distance of the remaining $K$ elements by a factor depending on $C(N)$.} there exists some $1\leq K \leq N-1$ and $\tilde{h} \geq \frac{3\hat{h}}{2}$ such that $\tilde{h} \leq C(N) \hat{h}$, $\tilde{h} \leq \frac{1}{4} \min_{1 \leq j \neq k \leq K} |\tilde{q}_j - \tilde{q}_k|$, and provided that $\frac{\hat{h}}{R_2}$ is sufficiently small, 
\begin{equation}
    \label{E:elementary-disjoint}
\bigcup_{j=1}^N U^{\hat{h}/2}_{R_2}(\tilde{q}_j) \subset \bigcup_{j=1}^{K} U^{\tilde{h}}_{(1+  \frac{\hat{h}}{2R_2})R_2}(\tilde{q}_j)
\end{equation}
which is the key to the induction argument. Observe that by \eqref{E:slabbed-ball-estimate-1}, we have for each $j=1,\cdots,N$ and all sufficiently large $i$ that for any $0<h \leq \frac{\hat{h}}{4}$,
\[ \frac{\|W_i\|(U^{h}_R(\tilde{q}_j))}{\omega_{n-1} R^{n-1}} \leq (1+C(n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0,\Lambda) r_i s_iR_2) \frac{\|W_i\|(U^{\hat{h}/2}_{R_2}(\tilde{q}_j))}{\omega_{n-1} R_2^{n-1}} + \frac{4}{3} \tau \left(\frac{2R_2}{\hat{h}}\right)^{n-1} \]
provided that $0 < R < R_2 \leq 2$. Summing over $j=1,\cdots,N$, using \eqref{E:elementary-disjoint} and noting that the family $\{ U^{\hat{h}/2}_{R_2}(\tilde{q}_j) \}_{j=1}^N$ are disjoint, we obtain by setting $R_2= \min\{1, \tau^{-\frac{1}{2(n-1)}} \hat{h}\}$ and $\epsilon=\tau^{\frac{1}{2(n-1)}}/2$ (recall $\tilde{q}_j \in U_\epsilon(T_p \mathbf{C})$), for all sufficiently large $i$ (depending on $\tau, n, i_0,k_0,\kappa_0,\Lambda$)
\begin{eqnarray*}
   && \sum_{j=1}^N  \frac{\|W_i\|(U^{h}_R(\tilde{q}_j))}{\omega_{n-1} R^{n-1}} \leq (1+C(n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0,\Lambda) r_i s_i R_2) \sum_{j=1}^{K} \frac{\|W_i\|(U^{\tilde{h}}_{(1+  \frac{\hat{h}}{2R_2}) R_2} (\tilde{q}_j)) }{\omega_{n-1}  R_2^{n-1}} +  \frac{4}{3} N \tau \left(\frac{2R_2}{\hat{h}}\right)^{n-1} \\
& &\leq (1+C(n,i_0,k_0,\kappa_0,\Lambda) r_i s_i) \left(1+ \frac{\hat{h}}{2R_2} \right)^{n-1} \sum_{j=1}^{K} \frac{\|W_i\|(U^{\tilde{h}}_{(1+  \frac{\hat{h}}{2R_2}) R_2} (\tilde{q}_j)) }{\omega_{n-1} \left( (1+ \frac{\hat{h}}{2R_2}) R_2 \right)^{n-1}} +  \frac{4}{3} N \tau \left(\frac{2R_2}{\hat{h}}\right)^{n-1} \\
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              
&&\leq (1+C(n,N) \tau^{\frac{1}{2(n-1)}})  \frac{\|W_i\|(B_2^\delta(0))}{\omega_{n-1} 2^{n-1}} +  C(n,N) \tau^{1/2}
\end{eqnarray*} 
for any $0<R<\min\{2,\tau^{-\frac{1}{2(n-1)}} C(N) h\}$. This finishes the proof of \eqref{E:multi-layer-monotonicity}. Finally, \eqref{E:multi-layer-monotonicity} clearly implies the claim by first choosing $\tau$ small (depending on $\gamma$, $n$ and $N$) and then letting $R \to 0$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- remark-11: body_md -->
\label{R:interior-tangent-cones}
    By \eqref{E:1st-variation-fbv-stationary}, if $x \in \operatorname{spt}\|V\|\cap \Omega \cap U$ (i.e. $x$ is an \textit{interior point}), then the varifold $V$ is stationary in a neighbourhood of $x$ away from $\partial \Omega$, and we can proceed with the same arguments above (cf. e.g. \cite[\S 42]{Simon1983}), so in particular $\operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)\neq\emptyset$ and any $\mathbf{C} \in\operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$ is an integer-rectifiable cone in $T_x\widetilde{M}$, which is stationary in $T_x\widetilde{M}$ provided that $V \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(U)$.
<!-- end remark-11 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
Let $V \in \mathbf{V}_{n-1}(U)$ be a varifold with free boundary in $\partial \Omega \cap U$, $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \subset \overline{\Omega} \cap U$ and $\|H^{tan}_V\|_{L^\infty(U)} \leq \Lambda$. Suppose $\mathbf C\in\operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$, where $x \in \partial \Omega$, satisfies $\operatorname{spt} \|C\|\subset T_x \partial \Omega$. Then, $\mathbf{C}=m |T_x \partial \Omega|$ for some positive $m\in \mathbb{R}$ in $T_x \widetilde{M}$. If $V \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(U)$, then $m \in \mathbb{N}$.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- remark-12: body_md -->
Note that some of the statements in this subsection hold in the more general setting of free boundary varifold with $H^{tan}_V\in L^p(U;\|V\|)$ for $p > n-1$ (see, for example, \cite{Masi2020}).
<!-- end remark-12 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{D:reg(V)-sing(V)}
Let $V\in\mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(U)$ be an integral free boundary stationary varifold in $\Omega \cap U$ with free boundary in $\partial\Omega\cap U$ and $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \subset \overline{\Omega} \cap U$. We define the \textit{regular set} $\operatorname{reg}(V)$ of $V$ to be
\begin{equation}
    \operatorname{reg}(V):=\bigg\{x \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\|:
        \begin{array}{l}
            \textrm{there exists \(r>0\) such that \(\operatorname{spt}\|V\|\cap B_{r}(x)\) is an embedded}
            \\
            \textrm{$C^\infty$ hypersurface with (possibly empty) boundary in \(\partial \Omega \cap U\)}
        \end{array}\bigg\}.
    \end{equation}
Moreover, we define its complement to be the \textit{singular set} $\operatorname{sing}(V)$ of $V$ by
\begin{equation}
    \operatorname{sing}(V):= \operatorname{spt} \|V\|\setminus\operatorname{reg}(V).
\end{equation}
The singular set can be further subdivided into its boundary part
\begin{equation}
    \operatorname{sing}_b(V)\coloneqq\operatorname{sing}(V)\cap \partial\Omega,
\end{equation}
and its interior part 
\begin{equation}
    \operatorname{sing}_i(V)\coloneqq \operatorname{sing}(V)\cap \Omega.
\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- remark-13: body_md -->
Note that by definition, $\operatorname{reg}(V)$ is a relatively open subset of $\operatorname{spt} \|V\|$. Hence, $\operatorname{sing}(V)$ is a relatively closed subset of $\operatorname{spt} \|V\|$. However, while $\operatorname{sing}_b(V)$ is also a relatively closed subset, $\operatorname{sing}_i(V)$ may not be. This simple but subtle observation is important in our analysis to follow.
<!-- end remark-13 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{P:Allard}
    There exists a small constant $\epsilon=\epsilon(n)\in(0,1)$ such that the following holds: given any free boundary stationary integral varifold $V\in\mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(U)$ with free boundary in $\partial\Omega\cap U$ and $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \subset \overline{\Omega} \cap U$, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \{x \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\|: 1 \leq \overline{\Theta}_V(x)<1+\epsilon\}\subset \operatorname{reg}(V).
    \end{equation}
    In other words, any singular point must have reflected density $\geq 1+\epsilon$. As a consequence, $\operatorname{reg}(V)$ is an open and dense subset of $\operatorname{spt} \|V\|$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- remark-14: body_md -->
In particular, if an interior point $x \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\| \cap \Omega$ has a tangent cone which is a multiplicity one plane, then $\overline{\Theta}_V(x)=\Theta_V(x)=1$ and thus $B_r(x) \cap \operatorname{spt} \|V\|$ is a smoothly embedded minimal hypersurface without boundary for some $r>0$. On the other hand, if a boundary point $x \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\| \cap \partial \Omega$ has a tangent cone which is a multiplicity one half-plane orthogonal to $T_x \partial \Omega$, then $\overline{\Theta}_V(x)=2\Theta_V(x)=1$ and thus $B_r(x) \cap \operatorname{spt} \|V\|$ is a smoothly embedded minimal hypersurface with non-empty free boundary lying on $\partial \Omega \cap U$ for some $r>0$.
<!-- end remark-14 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{D:spine}
Let $\mathbf{C}\in\mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ be an integer-rectifiable cone (in the sense that $(\eta_{0,r})_\sharp \mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}$ for all $r>0$). We define the \textit{spine} $S(\mathbf{C})$ of $\mathbf{C}$ to be the translation invariance set of $\mathbf{C}$, namely
\begin{equation}
    S(\mathbf{C})\coloneqq \{p\in \mathbb{R}^n:(\eta_{p,1})_\sharp\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}\}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{L:spine-boundary}
Let $\mathbf{C}\in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ be an integer-rectifiable cone with free boundary in $\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$ and $\operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}\| \subset \mathbb{R}^n_+$. Then $S(\mathbf{C})$ is a linear subspace contained in $\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$. In particular,
\begin{equation}
    S(\mathbf{C})=\{p \in \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+ \; : \; (\eta_{p,1})_\sharp\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}\}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, we have $(\eta_{p,1})_\sharp \|\delta \mathbf{C}\|_{sing}=\|\delta \mathbf{C}\|_{sing}$ for all $p \in S(\mathbf{C})$.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
It suffices to show that $S(\mathbf{C})$ is actually contained inside the hyperplane $\partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$. It follows from the simple observation $S(\mathbf{C}) \subset \operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}\|$ (as $0 \in \operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}\|$ since $\mathbf{C}$ is a cone) and the assumption that $\operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}\| \subset \mathbb{R}^n_+$. Hence, being a subspace lying inside the half-space forces $S(\mathbf{C}) \subset \partial \mathbb{R}^n_+$. The last assertion follows readily from Proposition \ref{P:free-boundary-characterization}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
Let $V \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(U)$ be a free boundary stationary varifold with free boundary in $\partial \Omega \cap U$ and $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \subset \overline{\Omega} \cap U$. For each $j\in\{0, 1,\dots, n-1\}$, we define the \emph{$j$-stratum} $\mathcal{S}_j$ of the singular set as follows:
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{S}_j := \{x \in \operatorname{sing}(V) \; : \; \dim S(\widehat{\mathbf{C}})\leq j \text{ for all } \mathbf{C} \in \operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)\}
\end{equation}
where for each tangent cone $\mathbf{C} \in \operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$, the \emph{modified cone} $\widehat{\mathbf{C}} \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(T_x \widetilde{M})$ is defined as
    \begin{equation}
        \widehat{\mathbf{C}}:= \left\{\begin{array}{cl}
        \mathbf{C} & \text{if $x \in \Omega$,} \\ 
        \mathbf{C}+(\theta_{-\nu(x)})_\sharp \mathbf{C} & \text{if $x \in \partial \Omega$.}
        \end{array}\right.
    \end{equation}
In other words, $\widehat{\mathbf{C}}$ is either the cone $\mathbf{C}$ itself or the doubled cone $\overline{\mathbf{C}}$ depending on whether the cone is based at an interior or a boundary point $x \in \operatorname{spt} \|V\|$. Note that in either case $\widehat{\mathbf{C}}$ is stationary (without free boundary!) in $T_p\widetilde{M}$ and $\Theta_{\widehat{\mathbf{C}}}(0)=\overline{\Theta}_V(x)$. From the definition we clearly have
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{S}_0\subset\mathcal{S}_1\subset\dots\subset\mathcal{S}_{n-1}=\operatorname{sing}(V).
\end{equation}
For each stratum $\mathcal{S}_j$, it is  useful to define the following subset of $\mathcal{S}_j$:
\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_j := \mathcal{S}_j\setminus\mathcal{S}_{j-1}=\left\{x \in \operatorname{sing}(V) \; : \; \max_{\mathbf{C} \in \operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)} \dim(S(\widehat{\mathbf{C}})) = j \right\}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{Ex:boundary-classical-cones}
Consider the following union of two half-planes 
\[ V_1=|\{(x,y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 \; : \; |x|=|y| \text{ and } y \geq 0\}| \times \mathbb{R}^{n-2} \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(\mathbb{R}^n)\]
which is a free boundary stationary varifold contained in the half-space $\mathbb{R}^n_+$, in this case we have 
\[ \operatorname{sing} (V_1)=\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n-2}=\{0\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n-2}.\] 
Perhaps a more interesting example is 
\[ V_2= \left( 2 | \{(x,y):y=\sqrt{3}x, \; y \geq 0  \} | +  |\{(x,y):x \leq 0, \; y=0\}| \right) \times \mathbb{R}^{n-2} \in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(\mathbb{R}^n)\]
which is also a free boundary stationary varifold in the half-space $\mathbb{R}^n_+$, in this case we still have 
\[ \operatorname{sing} (V_2)=\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n-2}=\{0\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n-2}.\]
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- remark-15: body_md -->
\label{R:cones-large-spine}
Before we state the main result (Proposition \ref{P:stratification}) concerning estimates on the Hausdorff dimension of each stratum of the singular set, we first take a closer look at the $j$-stratum for $j \geq n-3$, which will be most relevant for use in our paper. In our discussions below, let $x \in \operatorname{sing} (V)$ and $\mathbf{C} \in \operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$. Recall that the modified cone $\widehat{\mathbf{C}}$ is always a stationary integral cone in $T_p\widetilde{M}$ by Proposition \ref{P:tangent cones FB stationary varifolds} and Lemma \ref{L:reflection stationary}.
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
    \item If $\dim(S(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}))=n-1$ (i.e. $x \in \widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n-1}$), then the classical constancy theorem says that $\widehat{\mathbf{C}}= m |P|$ for some hyperplane $P \in \mathbf{G}_x(n,n-1)$ and $m  \in \mathbb{N}$. In fact, $m \geq 2$ by Allard's regularity (Proposition \ref{P:Allard}). If $x \in \operatorname{sing}_i(V)$ is an interior singular point, then clearly $\mathbf{C}=m|P|$. If $x \in \operatorname{sing}_b(V)$ is a boundary singular point, then we have the following dichotomy:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item either $P=T_x \partial\Omega$ and $\mathbf{C}=m|T_x \partial \Omega|$ for some $m \in  \mathbb{N}$ (possibly $m=1$);
        \item or $P \perp T_x \partial \Omega$ and $\mathbf{C}=m|P_+|$ for some $m \in  \mathbb{N}$ with $m \geq 2$ and $P_+:=P \cap T^+_x \widetilde{M}$.
    \end{itemize}
    
    \item If $\dim(S(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}))=n-2$, then by \eqref{E:cone-splitting} we have up to rotations the splitting 
    \begin{equation}
        \label{E:interior-classical-cone}
    \widehat{\mathbf{C}}=\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_0 \times S(\widehat{\mathbf{C}})\cong \widehat{\mathbf{C}}_0 \times \mathbb{R}^{n-2},
    \end{equation} 
    where $\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_0$ is a $1$-dimensional stationary integral cone in $\mathbb{R}^2$. By \cite{Allard-Almgren1976}, $\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_0$ consists of a finite union of rays emanating from the origin, each ray is counted with integer multiplicities satisfying the stationarity condition at the origin. Cones of the form \eqref{E:interior-classical-cone} are often referred to in the literature (c.f. \cite{Wick14}) as \textit{(interior) classical tangent cones}. If $x \in \operatorname{sing}_i(V)$ is an interior singular point, then $\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_0$ must consist of at least three rays. If $x \in \operatorname{sing}_b(V)$ is a boundary singular point, then we have the following dichotomy by \eqref{E:dichotomy-cones}:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item either $S(\widehat{\mathbf{C}})=S(\mathbf{C})\subset T_x \partial \Omega$, in which case 
        \begin{equation}
        \label{E:boundary-classical-cone}
        \mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}_0 \times S(\mathbf{C})
        \end{equation}
        where $\mathbf{C}_0$ is a $1$-dimensional free boundary stationary integral cone contained in the plane $\mathbb{R}^2 \cong \operatorname{span} \{\nu(x),e\}$ with $\operatorname{spt} \|\mathbf{C}_0\| \subset \mathbb{R}^2_+$. Here, $e \in T_x \partial \Omega$ is a unit vector perpendicular to $S(\mathbf{C})$ and $\mathbb{R}^2_+:=\{p \in \mathbb{R}^2 : p \cdot \nu(x) \leq 0\}$. We shall refer to cones of the form \eqref{E:boundary-classical-cone} as \emph{boundary classical tangent cone}.
        \item or $S(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}) \perp T_x \partial \Omega$, in which case $S(\widehat{\mathbf{C}})=S(\mathbf{C})\times \mathbb{R} \nu(x)$ so $\dim(S(\mathbf{C}))=n-3$. In fact, 
        \begin{equation}
            \label{E:half-classical-cone}
        \mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}_0 \times S(\mathbf{C}) \times \mathbb{R}_+ \nu(x)
        \end{equation} 
        where $\mathbb{R}_+ \nu(x)$ denotes the ray $\{-r \nu(x):r \geq 0\}$ along $-\nu(x)$ that points into $\Omega$, and $\mathbf{C}_0$ is a $1$-dimensional stationary (without free boundary!) integral cone contained in a $2$-dimensional subspace of $T_x \partial \Omega$. We shall refer to cones of the form \eqref{E:half-classical-cone} \emph{half classical tangent cone}.
    \end{itemize}
    
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            
                     
                                
                  
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end remark-15 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{P:stratification}
    Let $V\in \mathbf{IV}_{n-1}(U)$ be a free boundary stationary integral varifold with free boundary in $\partial \Omega \cap U$ and $\operatorname{spt} \|V\| \subset \overline{\Omega} \cap U$. Then, for all $j\in\{0,1,\dots, n-1\}$
    \begin{equation}
        \dim_\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}_j\leq j,
    \end{equation}
    where $\dim_\mathcal{H}$ denotes the Hausdorff dimension.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The proof is essentially the same as in the interior case (see e.g. \cite[Theorem 4]{White1997}). We shall follow the same structure as in \cite[\S 3.4]{Simon1996}, where an analogous result is proved in the context of energy-minimizing harmonic maps. 

The interior stratification theorem gives $\dim_\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{S}_j\cap\Omega\cap U)\leq j$: indeed, $V$ is stationary in each ball compactly contained in $\Omega\cap U$, and we can cover $\Omega\cap U$ by countably many such balls. It therefore remains to prove $\dim_\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{S}_j\cap\partial\Omega\cap U)\leq j$.


Let us fix $j\in\{0, 1, \dots, n-1\}$ and $\epsilon>0$. We shall say that a subset $A\subset \mathbb{R}^n$ has the $\epsilon$\textit{-approximation property} if there exists some $\rho_0>0$ such that for all $y\in A$ and $\rho \in (0, \rho_0]$, there is a $j$-dimensional subspace $W_{y, \rho}$ of $\mathbb{R}^n$ such that 
\begin{equation}
    \eta_{y, \rho}(A \cap B_\rho(y))\subset U_\epsilon(W_{y,\rho})
\end{equation}
where $\eta_{y,\rho}$ denotes the homothety map in \eqref{E:homothety} and $U_\epsilon(W_{y,\rho})$ denotes the $\epsilon$-open neighbourhood of $W_{y,\rho}$ in $\mathbb{R}^n$. By \cite[\S 3.4, Lemma 3]{Simon1996}, there exists a positive function $\beta:(0,+\infty) \to (0,+\infty)$ such that $\lim_{\epsilon \to 0}\beta(\epsilon)=0$ and $\mathcal{H}^{j+\beta(\epsilon)}(A)=0$ whenever $A \subset \mathbb{R}^n$ satisfies the $\epsilon$-approximation property.

Thus, it suffices to cover $\mathcal{S}_j\cap\partial\Omega\cap U$, in local coordinates, by countably many sets with the $C\epsilon$-approximation property, where $C$ is independent of $\epsilon$. We use ambient geodesic normal coordinates throughout; the transfer to fixed coordinate charts is justified in Step 2. The main arguments can be divided into two steps.

\vspace{1ex}

\noindent\textit{Step 1: Show that the ``good density'' points accumulate around a $j$-dimensional subspace.}

\vspace{1ex}

Take any $x \in \mathcal{S}_j\cap\partial\Omega\cap U$. We claim that there exists $\overline{\rho}=\overline{\rho}(x,\epsilon,V)\in(0,i_0/2)$, with $B_{\overline{\rho}}(x)\subset U$, such that for all $\rho \in(0, \overline{\rho}]$ there exists a $j$-dimensional subspace $W_{x,\rho} \subset T_x\partial\Omega$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{E:stratification-proof-key-claim}
    \eta_{0,\rho}\left(\exp_x^{-1}(\{y \in B_\rho(x)\cap\partial\Omega:\Theta_V(y) \geq \Theta_V(x)-\overline{\rho}\}) \right) \subset U_\epsilon(W_{x,\rho}).
\end{equation}
Here $U_\epsilon(W_{x,\rho})$ is the neighbourhood in $T_x\widetilde{M}$, as above; the image of $\partial\Omega$ under $\exp_x^{-1}$ need not lie in $T_x\partial\Omega$.

We will establish the claim by contradiction. Suppose the claim is false. Then there exist $x \in \mathcal{S}_j\cap\partial\Omega\cap U$ and sequences $\overline{\rho}_i \to 0$ and $\rho_i \in (0,\overline{\rho}_i]$ such that for each $i$
\begin{equation}\label{E:stratification-proof-failed-inclusion}
    \eta_{0,\rho_i}\left(\exp_x^{-1}(\{y \in B_{\rho_i}(x)\cap\partial\Omega:\Theta_V(y) \geq \Theta_V(x)-\overline{\rho}_i\}) \right) \not \subset U_\epsilon(W)
\end{equation}
for \emph{any} $j$-dimensional subspace $W \subset T_x\partial\Omega$. Up to passing to a subsequence, we can assume that $V_i := (\eta_{0,\rho_i})_\sharp (\exp_x^{-1})_\sharp V \to \mathbf{C} \in \operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$ as varifolds. Since $x \in \mathcal{S}_j$, we know that $\dim S(\widehat{\mathbf{C}}) \leq j$, and hence $\dim S(\mathbf{C})\leq j$ by \eqref{E:spine-inclusion}. Choose a $j$-dimensional subspace $W \subset T_x\partial\Omega$ containing $S(\mathbf{C})$. By \eqref{E:stratification-proof-failed-inclusion}, there exist $y_i \in B_{\rho_i}(x)\cap\partial\Omega$ such that, with $q_i:=\eta_{0,\rho_i}(\exp_x^{-1}(y_i)) \in B^\delta_1(0)$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{E:stratification-proof-failed-inclusion-2}
\Theta_V(y_i) \geq \Theta_V(x) - \overline{\rho}_i= \Theta_{\mathbf{C}}(0) - \overline{\rho}_i,
\end{equation}
but $q_i \notin U_\epsilon(W)$. After passing to a subsequence, we can also assume
\[
q_i\to q\in\bigl(\overline{B^\delta_1(0)}\cap T_x\partial\Omega\bigr)\setminus U_\epsilon(W),
\]
since the rescaled boundaries $\eta_{0,\rho_i}(\exp_x^{-1}(\partial\Omega))$ converge locally smoothly to $T_x\partial\Omega$. By \eqref{E:stratification-proof-failed-inclusion-2}, $\liminf_{i\to\infty}\Theta_V(y_i)\geq\Theta_\mathbf{C}(0)$. We claim that
\begin{equation}
    \label{E:upper-semi-continuity-density-1}
\Theta_{\mathbf{C}}(q) \geq \limsup_{i\to\infty}\Theta_V(y_i).
\end{equation}
Once the claim is established, $\Theta_\mathbf{C}(q)\geq\Theta_\mathbf{C}(0)$. Conversely, exact boundary monotonicity for $\mathbf{C}$ at $q\in T_x\partial\Omega$, together with conicality, gives $\Theta_\mathbf{C}(q)\leq\Theta_\mathbf{C}(0)$ by letting the radius tend to infinity. Thus \eqref{E:spine-max-density-boundary} implies $q\in S(\mathbf{C})\subset W$, a contradiction.

\vspace{1ex}

It remains to verify \eqref{E:upper-semi-continuity-density-1}. Fix $s>0$ and $a>0$. Since $q_i\to q$, the differential estimate \eqref{E:dexp-estimate} gives, for all sufficiently large $i$,
\[
\eta_{0,\rho_i}\bigl(\exp_x^{-1}(B_{\rho_i s}(y_i))\bigr)
\subset\overline{B^\delta_{(1+a)s}(q)}.
\]
